# American English production patterns — reviewed coverage catalog

Reviewed **2026-10-20** against the current local card sources.

This is the source-accounting and consolidation record for the complete replacement in [cards.md](cards.md), **not an Anki import or a list of mandatory new cards**. It replaces the original “Complete Reference” claim. The intended scope is everyday interaction, travel and services, relationships, workplace communication, technical discussion, and clear extended explanations. No finite phrase list establishes “almost 100% of AmE scenarios” or guarantees fluency.

Fluency also requires spontaneous interaction, listening comprehension, vocabulary, pronunciation, and adapting to context. ACTFL describes proficiency through real-world functions, contexts, accuracy, and the length/complexity of communication, rather than a percentage of memorized phrases. This catalog uses that functional perspective; it is not an ACTFL rating. [ACTFL Proficiency Guidelines overview](https://www.actfl.org/proficiency-guidelines-overview)

## What this review changed

- Accounted for **all 455 original entries**; consolidated them into **327 review units**, removing **128 separate duplicate/overlapping listings**. Original IDs remain under each unit.
- Added **51 coverage additions**, including expansions of existing material. Some join existing catalog units; they are **not 51 new cards**.
- The resulting **361 review units** include existing coverage, proposed expansions, practice references, lexical material, and possible new frames.
- Originally compared against **145 local `EN::Patterns` notes**, **603 validated Connection Patterns inventory items**, and **264 vocabulary master notes**. “Existing” means present in local sources, not a verified inspection of the user's desktop Anki collection.
- Corrected misleading goals, overly narrow templates, and AmE spelling. For example, `was supposed to` does not entail failure; `get back to someone` means contact them again; `keep up with` means maintain pace or stay informed.
- The initial audit left the deck unchanged. On 2026-10-20, the learner explicitly requested the complete replacement in `cards.md` for review. The replacement now merges and rewrites old card boundaries while preserving their useful coverage.

| Decision | Review units | Original entries assigned here | Meaning |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| REUSE | 39 | 56 | Existing Patterns card already teaches the useful construction; do not add another. |
| EXPAND | 49 | 67 | Improve the named existing card or catalog family with a missing form/example; not a new synonym card. |
| REFERENCE | 26 | 23 | Keep for transfer practice or use the current Connection/grammar note; no automatic new production card. |
| VOCABULARY | 89 | 101 | Primarily lexical; reuse the named vocabulary note or review there if still needed. |
| CANDIDATE | 158 | 208 | A potentially useful production task/form not adequately demonstrated by the identified Patterns cards; not yet an approved note. |

These are editorial decisions, not Anki tags, scheduling stages, or frequency rankings. A review unit may become no card, an edit to one card, or two short cards when its contrasts cannot fit clearly. **These historical review-unit counts are not card counts. The replacement below contains 280 complete cards; 12 conversation-glue cards, 2 topic-framing cards, and 29 published-material gap cards added on 2026-10-21 bring `cards.md` to 323.**

## Implemented replacement for learner review

The learner authorized consolidation and full source authoring on **2026-10-20**. `cards.md` now contains **280 complete cards**, and `anki-import.tsv` is rebuilt from that source. The learner has not yet accepted every resulting card; this is the complete version to review. No desktop Anki collection has been modified.

- All **145 old cards** are accounted for in the index below. Old standalone Fronts may disappear because their useful patterns are now shown together.
- All **361 catalog review units** have a **Replacement disposition**: **272** route to complete cards; **89** are intentionally not separate cards because they are lexical material under the learner’s known-vocabulary assumption. These 89 include the lexical `apply for` reference; the useful hypothetical-discussion frame `Let's say / Suppose` was instead included.
- All **455 original source IDs** and **51 added coverage IDs** remain traceable exactly once in the catalog’s Sources fields.
- The nine reviewed omission/shortening cards are carried over unchanged. The replacement adds productive coverage beyond the catalog where needed, including negative/question formation, agreement, pronoun reference, adverbs, future schedules, particle placement, and background `with` phrases.
- New grouped comparisons include matched examples and meaningful usage boundaries. A repeated expression inside a distinct contrast is not automatically a duplicate note: `even though` has a different job in the fact-versus-hypothesis comparison than in the sentence-restructuring comparison.

N001–N323 identify the current reading order only. Their links open the complete card in `cards.md`; they are not Anki scheduling identities. The old P001–P145 index is a snapshot used to prove the migration, not the current deck inventory.

The coverage review checks communicative functions and sentence-building systems. It does **not** measure a percentage of all AmE patterns or demonstrate the learner’s spontaneous mastery. Rare literary constructions, specialist legal syntax, pronunciation, and word-specific collocations remain outside this deck’s target.

## Belief and concern expressions: follow-up check

Checked against the actual source on 2026-10-20 after the learner asked about omissions. `I believe`, `I assume`, and `I suspect` were already explicit production frames. `I presume` and conversational `I bet / I'll bet` were missing; they were added to the then-current belief/assumption cards. The subsequent local-reference audit separates tentative views, assumptions, strong confidence, and conversational bet into clearer families. `I'm concerned that/about`, `It concerns me that`, and `My concern is that` were added to the worry/reaction family; the subsequent audit gives worry its own coherent card. The existing Connection Patterns cards C03-003 and C03-063 already teach narrower complement choices; the Patterns card supplies complete utterances with original examples.

This check exposes a limitation of the earlier audit: mapping communication functions and old source entries does not establish that most commonly used expression variants have been included. No representative corpus-frequency or external-inventory coverage percentage has been established. The three revised cards close the named gaps; they are not proof that all other families are exhaustive. That check left 186 cards; the later local-reference audit below now brings the draft to 280.

Usage references: [Cambridge assume/presume alternatives](https://dictionary.cambridge.org/us/thesaurus/assume), [Collins on conversational I bet](https://www.collinsdictionary.com/us/dictionary/english/i-bet-ill-bet-you-can-bet), and [Cambridge on concern](https://dictionary.cambridge.org/us/dictionary/english/concern?q=concerns). Definitions were checked; example sentences in the cards are original. Assume and presume overlap, and concern expresses worry here rather than a probability judgment.

## Conversation glue addition — 2026-10-21

After reviewing coverage for spontaneous speaking, the learner asked for short interaction frames that the grammar-reference audit did not target: agreeing with the same experience, passing the turn back, listening responses, reactions to news, turn openers, spoken markers, softeners, vague endings, quotative `be like`, strong impossibility, and handing over a decision. These are ordinary production cards in their own section; they do not change the 2026-10-20 audit decisions above.

`So/Neither + helper + I` overlaps with the repeated-action omission card, which owns the omission operation in coordination (`and so does Daniel`). The new card teaches the conversational reply and its choice against `me too / me neither`; it does not reteach the omission rule.

## Topic-framing addition — 2026-10-21

The learner asked whether reference expressions such as `regarding` and `according to` were covered. `according to`, `in terms of`, `speaking of`, and `given` already had production homes, and `regarding` appeared only as an email-opening topic. General topic markers were missing, so two cards were added: a spoken/neutral group and a professional/written group. Both Backs state the boundary with `in terms of`; `as far as I am concerned` remains on the opinion card. `based on` and `in light of / in view of` remain owned by the Connection Patterns deck.

| --- | --- |

## Published-material gap comparison — 2026-10-21

The learner asked for a comparison with well-known published inventories. Read-only research compared the deck with the British Council–EAQUALS *Core Inventory for General English* (2nd ed., B1–C1), the PHRASE List, the Academic Formulas List (spoken and core), Shin & Nation (2008, top 100), Biber, Conrad & Cortes (2004), the contents of Cambridge *Touchstone* (2nd ed. Levels 1–3; Level 4 from an unofficial copy), *Viewpoint* 1–2, *Interchange* 5th ed., and *Passages* 3rd ed., plus *American English File* Practical English scripts (3rd ed. Levels 2–3; Level 4 dialogue of uncertain edition). Every proposed gap was rechecked against `cards.md` and the Connection Patterns sources before inclusion.

The learner chose to consolidate both priority tiers and add everything. Grouping followed the existing test: forms share a card only when they express substantially the same message, clarify a genuine nearby contrast, or use the same construction. This produced 29 new cards (N295–N323) and extended 25 existing cards with 26 related forms. Three extended cards became overview cards because they now need more than three examples; they and the other extended overview cards still need the learner’s review as complete grouped cards.

Deliberately not added: `if only` (covered by `wish`), `could well`, the future perfect continuous, `That's what I'm saying`, `I was going to say`, `Don't tell me`, `figure out` (lexical), formal inversion such as `Should you need`, `Yeah, no`, and `Could you do me a favor` (the request card already has `Could I ask you a favor?`).

## Local reference audit — 2026-10-20

This follow-up reads the explanatory sections of **all 145 local Grammar in Use unit Markdown files**, plus the **eight Writing and four Speaking chapter Markdown transcriptions** in the Official Cambridge Guide. It checks sentence-building coverage and the relationships inside the groups. It does not claim to have listened to recordings, watched videos, solved all exercises, inspected every chart/scan, or audited the Reading/Listening skill chapters or practice tests. The local Grammar in Use lessons are the reviewed workspace reference; this audit does not establish their edition provenance or treat every simplification as an absolute rule.

The draft changed from **186 to 280 complete cards**: **51 existing card families revised**, including **34 split into clearer families**, and **49 additional production cards** added after checking existing ownership. The increase reflects clearer boundaries and documented gaps, not a target card count. All nine omission/shortening cards remain byte-for-byte unchanged. Every complete Front and Back is in [cards.md](cards.md); no additional preview file was created.

**Grouping test:** keep forms together when they express substantially the same message after restructuring, clarify a genuine nearby contrast, or supply the same reusable grammatical construction. Sharing only a topic, one word, or the fact that both occur in conversation is insufficient. A retained scenario overview must serve one clear task, such as navigating transit or resolving an order problem. Shared acceptable choices are explicit; no artificial confidence percentages or absolute synonym boundaries are taught.

- Opinion markers now include **in my opinion/view**, **from my perspective/point of view**, **personally**, and **as far as I am concerned**. Assumptions, tentative judgments, intuitions, strong confidence, and conversational **I bet** have separate homes.
- Separated examples from **in fact/actually**, sequencing from concluding, precautions from imagined scenarios, apologies from thanks, worry from positive reactions, difficulty from completion, and access from affordability/suitability.
- Split unrelated verb contrasts: **try**, **mean**, **remember/forget**, and **stop** no longer form a miscellaneous group. Object evaluations, caused states, and assigned names/roles are separate.
- Retained useful matched comparisons: **although/despite/however**, **used to/be used to**, **hope/wish**, **by/until**, active/passive causatives, and the reviewed location/direction overviews. A deliberate contrast does not claim the forms mean exactly the same thing.
- Added source-supported frames for **might as well**, **it’s time**, **need doing**, **I think so**, **own**, quantity and countability, noun modifiers, **still/no longer**, focus, email purposes/follow-ups, self-repair, and data descriptions.

**American-English adaptation:** own examples use AmE spelling and wording, including **in the hospital**, **in college**, conversational **do you have**, and ordinary simple-past alternatives for recent events. British **shall**, **needn’t**, institution/article conventions, and the source’s simplified **quite** scale are not silently imposed as universal AmE rules. Source files themselves are unchanged. Proper-name articles, individual lexical exceptions, rare formal variants, and exam-delivery practice are explicitly routed rather than treated as covered production cards.

The table records **core production coverage**, not every exercise answer or subsidiary grammar exception. A route can name a family with several useful cards; the scope column states the practical boundary. This gives a defensible broad foundation against these references, not a measured claim that a percentage of all American speech is covered.

## How to consolidate without losing useful differences

1. **Same form, different subject/tense/topic:** keep one construction. `I ended up working late` and `I always end up checking email` do not require two cards.
2. **Same communicative job and substantial overlap:** show alternatives together, with a short practical difference only where needed. `I think` and `I believe` do not need an invented certainty ranking.
3. **Same relationship, different grammar:** compare the shapes using matched sentences. `although + clause` and `despite + noun/V-ing` are a useful contrast.
4. **Related topic, different relationship:** cross-reference instead of forcing synonyms. `whereas` compares facts; `on the other hand` balances considerations; `even if` introduces a hypothetical condition.
5. **A lexical item plus an ordinary object:** usually use its vocabulary note and Common patterns. `outperform + target` should not be a duplicate Patterns note merely because the example mentions a benchmark.
6. **Already taught in Connection Patterns:** retain that grammar owner. Add a Patterns frame only when it materially helps perform a complete speaking/writing task; do not copy all 603 connection notes.

The current two-field format remains authoritative: **Front = reusable frame(s); Back = short explanation and natural examples**. The learner prefers related alternatives together when changing the sentence structure preserves the intended message. Different grammatical categories alone are not a reason to split them. A small contrast can fit the ordinary two/three-example format; a larger coherent comparison can use the reviewed-overview exception. Definitions/IPA/synonym inventories from the vocabulary deck do not belong on a normal pattern card.

A replacement deck may merge, rewrite, or omit redundant old notes. Before declaring the replacement complete, map every existing Patterns card to retained coverage, a merged replacement, or an intentional omission with a reason. The old-card index now maps every one of the 145 source notes to complete replacement cards. These N-identifiers are document references, not persistent Anki note IDs. If scheduling preservation is desired, update notes using their existing identities. If the learner chooses fresh notes, restarting their scheduling is an acceptable trade-off. Importing a new TSV does not itself retire old notes; the final migration must explicitly handle them. [Anki's import documentation](https://docs.ankiweb.net/manual/importing/text-files#duplicates-and-updating)

## Research informing grouped comparisons

Research checked 2026-10-20. Comparing examples together, interleaving practice questions, and putting several targets on one Anki card are different interventions. The studies below do not directly compare this exact combined connector card with separate connector cards in Anki.

- **Case comparison:** Alfieri, Nokes-Malach, and Schunn (2013) synthesized 57 experiments and found an average learning advantage for comparison activities. This supports deliberately examining shared structure and differences, but it is broad evidence across tasks, not a test of this deck. [Paper](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/00461520.2013.775712)
- **Practice order and different outcomes:** Zhang (2023) studied requests and suggestions with 63 learners of English. Interleaved practice benefited pragmatic accuracy, whereas blocked practice benefited fluency. Alternating tasks is not equivalent to placing answers together on a card, and a method can help one outcome more than another. [Paper](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/studies-in-second-language-acquisition/article/effects-of-interleaved-and-blocked-corpusbased-practice-on-l2-pragmatic-development/5A4444ED013A13A681076AF3E6F974CB)
- **Related words can interfere:** Waring (1997) found a disadvantage for learning semantically related new word pairs together. The task used Japanese–artificial-word pairs; it does not establish that comparing familiar English connectors in complete sentences is harmful. It cautions against assuming that all synonym lists become easier merely by grouping them. [Paper](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0346251X97000134)

**Design judgment:** A compact shared-meaning card is a reasonable choice for this learner's connector family. Present matched sentences and make the required restructuring explicit. Grouping is justified by the production task, not by a claim that research proves one large card always wins. Before revealing the Back, produce a new sentence in each structural form; simply recognizing the displayed examples does not demonstrate that ability.

A combined card has one review outcome, so a weak form may cause already-known forms to be reviewed again. Split a group only when production or self-grading becomes cumbersome or one part repeatedly fails. There is no evidence-based fixed maximum number of expressions established by the studies above. If uncertain, compare a few matched families using equal study time and a delayed test with new sentences; look at accuracy, response time, and confusions rather than preference alone. Such a small personal trial is diagnostic, not a controlled proof.

## Worked consolidation: contrast and concession

**Revised proposal: put the basic `but / although / though / even though / despite / in spite of / however` alternatives on one grouped Patterns card.** Its single job is to express an unexpected contrast through different sentence structures. The comparison below describes parts of that card and neighboring decisions, not a rule that each row must be a separate card.

| Family / existing owner | Matched use | Useful distinction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| One grouped contrast card — expand/replace [P042](#existing-p042), drawing related material from [P067](#existing-p067) | I was tired, but I finished it. / Although/Though/Even though I was tired, I finished it. / Despite/In spite of being tired, I finished it. / I was tired. However, I finished it. | Shared message here; clause joining, dependent clause, noun/V-ing complement, or a separate statement. Even though adds emphasis. But has broader uses too, such as correcting a choice: not red but blue. |
| Fact versus hypothetical — [P043](#existing-p043) | Even though I'm tired, I'll finish it. / Even if I'm tired, I'll finish it. | The first presents tiredness as a fact; the second makes it a condition that will not change the outcome. |
| Nearby counterpoint alternatives — [P067](#existing-p067) | It's expensive. Still/Even so, I'd buy it. | Add as concise alternatives only if the group stays usable. Retain a separate That said card only if its qualification examples provide a useful distinct production target; otherwise absorb them and retire the redundant note. |
| Conversational afterthought — possible extension of the same grouped card | It's expensive. I'd buy it, though. | Sentence-final though works here; although/even though do not. It need not become a separate card merely because its position differs. |
| Compare two facts — [CONTRAST06](#contrast06) | I prefer remote work, whereas/while she prefers the office. | A direct comparison, without requiring a surprising result. |
| Balance considerations — [P004](#existing-p004) | Remote work saves commuting time. On the other hand, collaboration can be harder. | Weigh two sides; no need to say on the one hand every time. |

Grammar cross-check: [Cambridge on linking contrasting ideas](https://dictionaryblog.cambridge.org/2022/11/16/whereas-despite-and-nevertheless-ways-to-link-ideas-1/) and [Cambridge's American-English entry for though](https://dictionary.cambridge.org/us/dictionary/english/though). Examples here are original. Cambridge's grammar resources may describe both varieties; the examples and production choices here target AmE.
